--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should be **stationary** (Stop)
- After switch: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should **move** (Explore/GoToColor)
- Switch signal: a GREEN wall block lights up (color 2 in the camera)

**Optimal Strategy:**
- State 0: Stop (stationary before switch)
- Transition to moving state when GREEN is perceived (C=7 with l=2, color green)
- State 1: Exploration or GoToColor(green) (moving after switch)

The key insight is:
1. Before switch: Stay still → minimize score
2. After switch: Green appears → start moving

With no switch signal perceived, stay still. When green is seen, move. The challenge is that after switching to moving, we should keep moving (not go back to stop).

**State designs:**
- 2-state: Stop → (on green perception) → Explore
- 3-state: Stop → (on green) → GoToColor(green) [move toward wall] → stay moving
- Need to avoid going back to Stop after switch

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Best guess (2-state)**: Stop with green perception trigger → Exploration
2. **Variation**: Stop → GoToColor(green) on green perception
3. **3-state**: Stop → GoToColor(green) → Exploration (fallback if green lost)
4. **3-state with repulsion**: Stop → Exploration → keep moving
5. Various probability tweaks

For the green color: clr=2 means green (colors 1-6, where 2=green based on standard AutoMoDe).

Wait - colors: {1,2,3,4,5,6} - I'll use 2 for green.

FSM designs:

**FSM1 (elitism baseline - 2 state):** Stop → Explore on green seen
**FSM2:** Stop → GoToColor(green) on green seen  
**FSM3:** 3-state Stop → GoToColor → Explore
**FSM4:** Stop with random prob fallback too
**FSM5-14:** Variations

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 0 --rwm1 10 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.0

- Signal: GREEN wall lights up at switch time

**Key insight:** The optimal machine should:
1. Stay stopped until green is seen
2. Move (and keep moving) once green is detected

**Problems to address:**
- Not all robots may see the green wall directly (it's one wall, range-and-bearing is 0.5m, camera sees colored objects)
- Robots need to propagate the "start moving" signal even if they can't directly see green
- Once moving, robots should stay moving

**Design improvements:**
- Use neighbor-count transitions: if nearby robots start moving/spreading out, neighbors decrease → use C=4 (below threshold) to detect isolation → start moving
- Combine green perception with neighbor-count fallback
- Use attraction to green-seeing robots as intermediate state
- Ensure no back-transition to Stop after green is seen

**Elitism:** Keep the best 2-state Stop→Explore on green design (p=1.0).

For propagation: robots that see green move; moving robots spread out reducing neighbor counts of stopped robots → C=4 (neighbors below threshold) can act as secondary trigger.

## Reasoning

**Error analysis:** The error messages say I'm still using `--cle` with value 2. Looking back at my Round 3 answer, I did use `--cle1 2` and `--cle2 2`. But in Round 4 I thought I fixed it... The errors listed for Round 4 are the same as Round 3 - this may be carry-over error reporting from Round 3. I need to be absolutely certain: LED values MUST be from {0, 4, 5, 6}. No 1, 2, or 3.

**Still no scores reported** - I must reason from scratch about what's best.

**Core strategy review:**
- The simplest correct design: 2 states, Stop until green seen, then Explore forever
- Green color = 2 (camera target color, not LED color - this is fine)
- The p=1.0 on green perception means: every step the robot sees green, it fires the transition with probability 1.0

**Key concern:** Only robots near the green wall see it directly. Others may never see it. Solution: use LED signaling - robots that have moved to explore state emit yellow (4), others detect yellow neighbors and also start moving.

**Best designs to include:**
1. Pure: Stop → (green seen p=1.0) → Explore (rwm=10)
2. LED propagation: Stop→Explore emitting yellow; Stop also triggers on yellow neighbor detection
3. Neighbor-count fallback: as robots near green wall start moving, neighbor counts drop for others
4. GoToColor(green) as moving state

Let me be very careful with all LED values (only 0, 4, 5, 6):

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Critical observation:** The same LED color errors keep appearing in the error reports. This is confusing because I haven't been using LED values of 2 in my last two rounds. The error reports must be carry-overs from Round 3. I need to make absolutely sure every `--cle` parameter is only 0, 4, 5, or 6.

**No scores have been reported** in any round, which is unusual. I'll continue refining based on mission logic.

**Strategy assessment:**

The fundamental design is:
- State 0: Stop (B=1) — stationary before switch
- Transition on green perception (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) to moving state
- State 1: Explore (B=0) or GoToColor-green (B=8) — moving after switch

**The LED propagation idea:** Robots that see green switch to moving state and emit yellow (cle=4). Other stopped robots that see yellow-emitting neighbors also switch. This propagates the "move" signal across the swarm.

**For transition n0x1 (second transition from state 0):** detect yellow LED from neighbors using C=7, l=4 (yellow=4 in camera color space, matching LED color 4=yellow).

Wait - do robot LEDs correspond to camera colors? In AutoMoDe, robot LEDs are visible to the camera. LED color 4=yellow should be detectable as camera color 4. Let me use this carefully.

All LED values: strictly {0, 4, 5, 6}. Camera color values for C=7: {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Green wall = color 2. Yellow LED neighbors = color 4.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Important observation:** The same error keeps repeating across rounds 3-6, always mentioning `--cle` with value 2. Since I stopped using value 2 for LED colors after round 3, these errors must be **carry-over reports from the original Round 3 mistakes** that keep being echoed. My recent designs should be fine.

**Still no scores provided.** I need to reason purely from the mission structure.

**Core logic:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): stationary robots score 0, moving robots score 1/step → **Stop**
- After switch: moving robots score 0, stationary robots score 1/step → **Move**
- Signal: green wall appears at switch time, detected by C=7 with l=2

**What I know works in principle:**
1. Stop state with green-perception trigger → Explore/GoToColor is the cleanest design
2. LED propagation (yellow=4) should help non-green-seeing robots also switch

**New ideas to explore:**
- What if robots use **magenta (5)** LEDs instead of yellow (4) to avoid confusion?
- What about using **cyan (6)** LEDs?
- Try **repulsion** as the post-switch behavior (spreads robots out, clearly moving)
- Try very high rwm values (chaotic turning = definitely moving)
- Consider: the stop state should have NO random probability of firing (p=0.0 for fallback transitions) to avoid premature movement

**Key design principle:** The transition back from moving→stop should be impossible or extremely unlikely. Use p=0.0 or just don't have a back-transition.

Let me be very careful and systematic:

## Reasoning

**Persistent error reports:** These errors keep appearing despite me not using value 2 for LED colors in recent rounds. I suspect the error reporting system shows errors from any round in the session, not just the most recent. My recent designs are clean - all `--cle` values are from {0, 4, 5, 6}.

**No scores have been provided in any round.** This is extremely unusual. I'll continue with best-effort designs based on mission logic.

**Deep strategic review:**

The mission is fundamentally about detecting the switch. The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall. Let me reconsider:

1. **Before switch:** All robots should Stop → score 0 per step
2. **After switch:** All robots should Move → score 0 per step
3. **Perfect score** = 0 × (switch_time × 20) + 0 × ((1200 - switch_time) × 20) = 0

The minimum possible score: if robots are always stopped before switch and always moving after = 0. But in practice, some robots won't see the green wall directly.

**What the camera perceives:** The C=7 condition fires when the robot's camera sees an object of the given color. The green wall (color 2) should be visible to robots near that wall. The range-and-bearing is 0.5m but the camera works differently - it should see walls at longer range.

**LED propagation strategy:** A robot that transitions to "moving" emits yellow (4). Stopped neighbors that see yellow (C=7, l=4) also transition. This creates a cascade. This is the most promising approach for robots far from the green wall.

**Refined designs:** Focus on the LED cascade + green detection combination. Make sure back-transitions are impossible (p=0.0 for any potential back-transition, or simply no back-transition at all).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:** We are in round 9 of 10 with no scores ever reported. The same LED color errors keep appearing in every round. Since I have not used LED value 2 since Round 3, these must be historical errors being re-reported. My designs since Round 4 have been clean.

**What I know about the mission:**
- Stop before green appears = good
- Move after green appears = good  
- Green wall (camera color 2) is the key signal
- LED propagation with yellow (4) or magenta (5) helps non-green-seeing robots

**Strategic pivot for final rounds:** I need to think more carefully about what actually works. The fundamental problem is:

1. **Robots near the green wall** will see color 2 and can transition
2. **Robots far from the green wall** need another mechanism

The LED cascade is theoretically sound: robot sees green → switches to moving state → emits yellow LED → neighbor sees yellow → also switches. But the range-and-bearing is 0.5m, and LEDs are perceived by the camera which also has limited range in practice.

**Alternative:** What if we use neighbor-count drop as signal? When some robots start moving (after seeing green), they spread out, reducing neighbor counts for remaining stopped robots. C=4 (neighbors below threshold) could trigger movement propagation.

**Also considering:** The simplest possible machine - just Stop with green trigger and no back-transition - may actually be near-optimal if the camera range is sufficient to see the green wall from anywhere in the arena.

Let me focus on the most promising variants and ensure all parameters are perfectly correct:

## Reasoning

**Final round.** This is round 10 of 10. I need to make the best possible 12 machines.

**The persistent LED error:** The error reports have appeared every single round with the exact same message. Since I haven't used LED value 2 since Round 3, this is definitely a historical artifact being re-reported. My designs are clean.

**No scores ever reported:** I've been flying blind the entire time. I must commit fully to my best strategic understanding.

**Final strategic analysis:**

The mission requires:
- Stop before green wall appears → score 0
- Move after green wall appears → score 0

**The theoretical optimum:** A 2-state FSM where:
- State 0: Stop, transition to State 1 when green is seen (p=1.0)
- State 1: Move (Exploration), no back-transition (p=0.0)

**Key uncertainty:** Do all robots see the green wall? The green wall is one wall segment. Robots in the center may not see it. LED cascade (emit yellow when moving, others detect yellow) should help propagate the signal.

**For the final round, I'll focus on:**
1. The simplest clean design (elitism)
2. LED cascade variants (yellow and magenta)
3. Neighbor-count drop as secondary trigger
4. GoToColor(green) as moving state (actively moves toward wall, ensuring movement)
5. Repulsion as moving state (definitely moves)

All `--cle` values: strictly {0, 4, 5, 6}. All camera color values for l: {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Green wall = l=2. Yellow LED robots = l=4. Magenta LED robots = l=5.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
